# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

To mitigate the impact of climate change and strengthen resilience of the most vulnerable communities to adapt to extreme energy, Alternative Energy Foundation (AEF) has proposed a project to improve the livelihoods of women in rural areas through clean cooking solutions. The main objective is to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting clean cooking solutions for 10,000 households in Kenya.

The project will:

- Provide clean cooking solutions.
- Reduce indoor air pollution and deforestation.
- Improve health outcomes and economic opportunities for women.
- Promote sustainable development goals (SDGs).

The project aims to promote clean cooking solutions such as Tier 3 Improved Cooking Stoves (ICS) in 10,000 households. The project will be implemented over four years with an estimated budget of USD 2 million. The project will provide clean cooking solutions to 10,000 households and create 500 jobs.

The project also aims to address specific barriers that limit adoption of traditional efficient cooking practices by providing an efficient and affordable cooking solution. The government's initiative on ICS, the Clean Cooking Stoves Program (CCSP), will be integrated into the project. The project will change cooking patterns with wider usage of modern clean cooking solutions vs. traditional biomass-based cooking methods.

The project will also help to conserve the environment by reducing greenhouse gas emissions and improving air quality. The project will also promote women's empowerment, reduce indoor air pollution, and improve health outcomes for women and children.

The project is expected to replace traditional cooking (TCS) by Tier 3 ICS, which will primarily benefit women and children as women are mainly responsible for cooking in rural areas.

---

**Notes:**

- Tier 3 ICS has an efficiency of 50%. These stoves emit 2 g/h of carbon monoxide and 1.8 g/h of particulate matter.
- The project will not exceed an MOU signed during a field visit to FAO regarding the use of LPG as a clean cooking solution in Kenya.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate context (max. 100 words, approximately 2 pages)

Climate change impacts, such as changes in temperature and precipitation patterns, are expected to have significant effects on Nepal's economy and society. These changes will affect the agricultural sector, water resources, and human health. The impacts of climate change are expected to be more severe in the mountainous regions of Nepal.

## B.2 Project description

Nepal is a landlocked country bordered by India and China. Topographically, it is a domain of three ecological regions: namely Mountains, Hills and Terai (lowland plains). These are bordered by the Indian plains to the south and the Tibetan Plateau to the north. The Himalayan region comprises about 32% of the total area, including some parts of the Churia range (12-15 km wide). The mid-mountain region covers approximately 68% of the total land area, while the Terai region occupies around 9.41% of total area.

Access to clean energy cooking solutions is a massive problem for Nepal, including Terai, Nepal and mountain regions. About 70% of the population uses traditional biomass (wood, agricultural waste) as their primary source of fuel. This leads to indoor air pollution, which causes respiratory diseases such as pneumonia, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), lung cancer, and stroke. The use of biomass also contributes to deforestation and soil erosion.

## B.3 Project objectives

The project is not based on FMO project (including a Regional Climate Region in Nepal) and FAO against the Climate Change Adaptation Program. The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting renewable energy sources such as solar, wind, and hydro power. It also seeks to improve energy efficiency and promote sustainable transportation.

## B.4 Project activities

The project will involve the following activities:

- Conducting a baseline assessment of the current energy situation in Nepal.
- Developing a strategy for promoting clean energy cooking solutions.
- Providing financial support to households for purchasing clean energy cooking devices.
- Implementing awareness campaigns to educate the public about the benefits of clean energy cooking solutions.

## B.5 Project outcomes

The expected outcomes of the project are:

- A significant reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from the energy sector.
- Improved health outcomes for households that switch to clean energy cooking solutions.
- Increased access to affordable and reliable energy services for rural communities.
- Enhanced energy security and resilience to climate change.

## B.6 Project implementation

The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with the following milestones:

- Year 1: Conduct baseline assessment and develop strategy.
- Year 2: Launch awareness campaigns and provide initial financial support to households.
- Years 3-5: Continue providing financial support to households and monitor progress towards outcomes.

## B.7 Project budget

The total budget for the project is $X million, with the following breakdown:

- Baseline assessment: $Y
- Strategy development: $Z
- Awareness campaigns: $W
- Financial support to households: $V
- Monitoring and evaluation: $U
- Contingency fund: $T

## B.8 Project risks

The project faces several risks, including:

- Political instability in Nepal.
- Changes in international energy prices.
- Delays in the implementation of government policies related to clean energy.

## B.9 Project sustainability

To ensure the sustainability of the project, the following measures will be taken:

- Building partnerships with local organizations and communities.
- Providing training and capacity building for local stakeholders.
- Developing a long-term strategy for the promotion of clean energy solutions in Nepal.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 1 OF 13

Household contributor is admitted to produce 25% of global emissions of black carbon, which is the second major contributor to climate change after carbon dioxide (CO2). The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates 8,700 deaths a year from indoor air pollution in Nepal.

![Figure 1: Proposed Location](image_url)

In Nepal, as cow dung are more easily available and free to collect, households rely heavily on dung cakes as cooking fuel. It is used by 25% of the population. This cooking pattern is typical in the Terai region (southern plains) where cattle are reared. The cattle population in these regions are at major 0.1% and 0.4% respectively. Dung cake is the most polluting fuel for cooking purposes. It emits more particulate matter than wood, coal, or agricultural waste. Households living in low-lying areas use cow dung as fuel. They live in a crowded area with poor ventilation, high humidity, and high temperature. The smoke from burning cow dung causes respiratory diseases such as pneumonia, bronchitis, asthma, and lung cancer.

## References
- WHO, 2016. Burning Opportunity: Clean Household Energy for Health, Business, Development, and Wellbeing of Women and Children.
- UNEP, 2017. New Perspectives on Clean Cooking.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 16 OF 111

![Figure 2: Energy ladder representing household cooking in Nepal](image_url)

While TOR is predominantly used for cooking in the rural areas, Liquid Petroleum Gas (LPG) is the most commonly used cooking technology in the urban areas. Due to its smoke-free operation, flexibility in use and available fuel options depending on this technology, there is a growing trend of LPG adoption among the urban households. However, it has been observed that only 14% of LPG consumption is even higher in the Terai region.

Even though LPG is a cleaner fuel compared to fuel wood and dung, as it does not produce smoke and contributes to indoor air quality, it is derived from fossil fuels, hence contributes to global GHG emissions. The share of LPG in the total energy mix has increased over time and reached 4.27% increase since 2005/06. This share represents an LPG input has not been able to replace fuel wood as a primary source of cooking fuel. This is due to the fact that LPG is only available for those who can afford it. It has also made heavy dependence on LPG order to strengthen its economy and reduce the pressure on forest resources. Nepal made its independent oil exploration in 2014, which was recently discovered in the western part of Nepal. The government is planning to start drilling in 2018.

Electrically, one big effort that Nepal could opt for is a transition to clean cooking solution. Though it has been observed that the use of electricity for cooking is very low (only 3%) due to the high cost of fuel and lack of access to electricity. Nepal was ranked under an acute power shortage but resulted in up to 16 hours of load shedding per day. However, the government is planning to increase its installed capacity to 1500 MW by 2020. Government of Nepal has announced a plan to provide free electricity for cooking to all households in the country by 2030. Currently, nearly 86% of the total population has access to electricity.

With an actual capacity of 10 MW, the country would generate around 60 GWh of electricity annually, which could be used for further industrialization and economic development.

At present, 80% of the nation's population is connected to national grid. Nepal has recently signed an electricity purchase agreement with India and is in the process of signing another one with Bangladesh. However, the country still faces challenges in providing reliable power supply to its citizens. The government has set a target of achieving universal access by 2030.

With the backing of an ambitious language in the Clean Cooking Solution for the (ICC4ALL) hosted in 2019, the Government of Nepal has endorsed investment proposals to obtain funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The Government of Nepal has also endorsed the improved solutions of cooking to 2030 (modular and open fire burning) with modern cooking stoves for 2050.

## G

### Biogas

There is an existing subsidy provision for building domestic biogas, however many households cannot afford the capital cost of building a biogas plant. Affordable biogas technology is about CCS. Furthermore, household cooking is a cultural practice that women are responsible for household chores.

The use of biogas can reduce the drudgery and health hazards associated with firewood collection. Biogas can be used as a clean fuel source in rural areas where electricity is not available. It also reduces the cost and health benefits of CCS through community and promotional activities. Also, it will reach the families of the poor households, the Tea region of Rwanda for five years period that will reduce the pressure on natural resources and improve the environment around the communities of the Tea Region. The use of biogas technology is a clean source of energy that can be used to replace firewood and charcoal. It also supports an enhanced rural and sustainable services and technology availability. The use of biogas technology will follow a natural course when the benefits are realized.

## 3. Financial Barriers

Most of the TOS are now going the lowest bidder, and they look optimal to win by EPC technology. Furthermore, they are usually deprived of normal banking finance, hence do not have enough capital to invest in EPC technology. The interest rate is also high for the poor households. For instance, the interest rate on an electric cooking stove loan from a microfinance institution is 20% per annum and the interest rate on a solar home system loan is 18% per annum. The average annual income of a household in Nepal is around USD 500. The repayment period is also coming to be very short (3-6 months) compared to the life span of the equipment. The low technology financing households are deprived of the benefits of the TOS.

After the project, high interest rates due to existing banking delivery mechanism will continue to prevail. The government can play a major role in addressing this issue by providing long-term loans at lower interest rate and also by creating a revolving fund for the poor households. Climate Fund and GOF (GAF) and other possible development partners could help in designing the loan scheme and also in providing the funds. The government should also consider a base line selection approach for cost effectiveness and robust payment to de-risk the investment (see section 4).

From the financial perspective, the biggest barrier seems to be the upfront capital requirement. This is the main reason why the TOS are not being adopted at scale. The Tariff is set by the government and the upfront capital required to install the TOS is high. The government can play a major role in addressing this issue through the following measures:

- Provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS.
- Provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS.
- Provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS.

The government can also provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS. The government can also provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS. The government can also provide subsidies to households for the upfront cost of the TOS.

## 4. Institutional Barriers

After the promulgation of new constitution in 2015, Nepal has become a federal republic, with country divided into seven provinces. The role of provincial governments is still evolving and there are many challenges ahead.

create rural municipalities, while new municipalities and metropolitan cities have also been created. In a similar vein, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) has been established to support developing countries in their efforts towards climate change mitigation and adaptation. The GCF is governed by the Local Government for Development of Renewable Energy (LGDRE). Key areas of focus include:

- Technical and financial capacity,
- Environmental sustainability,
- Decentralized human resources,
- Community-based renewable energy projects, procurement (element aggregation and bulk tendering approach), implementing and monitoring renewable energy technologies projects and programs.

The intended project should empower LGDs by heavily engaging them. Through this process, the project will ensure that local governments are able to effectively manage their own resources for climate change mitigation and adaptation. The project will also focus on periodic and annual planning, formulating appropriate policies and mechanisms.

Environmental considerations, etc., as relevant, that need to be addressed for a proposal (chapter of environmental considerations) to either be included in the project’s budget or not.

The primary problem that this project will address is increasing greenhouse emissions and induce air pollution due to inefficient cooking practices. This project aims to mitigate the problem by providing clean cooking stoves to households and communities, which are developed with an increased demand for fuel-efficient development.

While high dependence on biomass fuel brings different CCS and green use of LPG in increasing carbon emission, it is also true that it has a lot of benefits. It is a renewable source of energy.

### B1. Inadequate capital investment and operational cost
Most of the TCS users do not have access to banking facilities to borrow money. In the rural market, banks and financial institutions lack trust in the technology being utilized as TCS in these ECs. The new communities will need to partner with local microfinance institutions to provide loans for clean cooking stoves. The selection criteria for the green technology and the subsidized loan will be based on the community’s needs and the project’s objectives. This is to ensure that the users are not exploited by the financial institutions.

### B2. Technical and operational barrier pertaining to adoption of new CCS technology
Despite the high demand for clean cooking stoves, the lack of technical know-how and efficiency on supply side is still weak in most areas of the country, which is a major barrier to the adoption of clean cooking stoves. The government has been working with the private sector to develop additional local demand produced by ECA. Additionally, at household level, the project will support the development of local manufacturing capacity for CCS.

### B3. Lack of technical and project implementation skill at a subnational institutions
Most sub-national states do not possess the technical capacity to implement and supporting energy efficiency projects. This is a major barrier to the adoption of clean cooking stoves in these areas. The project will provide training and support to build this capacity.

### B4. Lack of awareness and limited access to CCS technology
There are not enough information, education, and communication (IEC) activities to raise awareness about the benefits of TCS usage and the availability of new technologies for clean cooking stoves. Most of the TCS users are not aware of the benefits of using TCS. Additionally, most of the LPG stove users are not familiar with the benefits of CCS technology.

### B5. Lacking technical standards approach for CCS
Because these relatively new technologies are introduced to the domestic consumers, currently there are not many manufacturers and suppliers of Tier 1 in YCS and CCS. The project will support the development of technical standards and quality assurance systems.

resources are scarce, even more so in the context of rural areas where large portion of the 3x5 CCS will be implemented. This may present a risk to consumers of adoption of CCS due to limited disbursement.

### BI funding disparity in CCS value chain and invisible opportunities and limited accessibility for women

Storms in an actual implementation (a) and (b) have been observed in other similar business areas. The lack of access to finance and the inability to participate in the value chain is a major barrier to women's participation in CCS. Furthermore, women's participation is related to decision making and income generating activities.

#### R1: Investment risk due to existing subsidy delivery mechanisms

Current subsidy delivery mechanisms are not designed to address the needs of smallholder farmers. This creates a challenge for the government to achieve big impact in a short period.

The major activities are based on five major areas:

1. **Increasing access to CCS by deployment of proposed technologies**
   - Deployment of CCS for scaling up the implementation targets. The activity involves procurement and distribution of CCS equipment to smallholder farmers.
   - Access to finance is a major risk factor for women's CCS adoption.

2. **Strengthening quality assurance and enhancing product standards**
   - Enhancing product standards, including assessments, surveys and analysis. Within this component, the activities will be focused on strengthening technical and operational standards of CCS equipment.
   - The activity will also address BI and R1 risks from women's view. The activity will address E2.

3. **Strengthening quality assurance mechanisms**
   - The activity will build focus on enhancing product standards for smallholder farmers to adopt CCS technologies.
   - The activity will address E2.

4. **Creating additional levels, enabling functional CCS market and increasing awareness of CCS**
   - The activity will enhance the capacity of smallholder farmers to adopt CCS technologies.
   - The activity will address E3.

5. **Capacity development of sub-national institutions**
   - Through the activity, sub-national institutions will be equipped to promote CCS promotion in the annual plans, formulating policies and regulations for CCS implementation.
   - The activity will also enhance product standards. The activity will address B1.

4. Strengthened supply chain and technical capacity will lead to sustainable adoption of the CCS.

## 8.3 Programme description (more than 2000 words, approximately 5 pages):

### Objective

The objective is to provide a comprehensive set of activities and solutions that will assist in the expected fund being used and sustainable results. Components include relevant to:

- **Objective:** To support the development of CCS technologies.
- **Target:** To reduce emissions by 20% by 2030.

This should be pursued with the following by Government and various stakeholders.

### The Programme

Integrating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean cooking solutions. This will include:

1. **Modern Bioenergy (AER) and Liquefied Petroleum Gas (LPG):** We continue to bring GHG emissions and strengthen the resilience of most vulnerable households.
2. **Electricity:** We continue to promote electricity as a reliable source of energy for clean cooking solutions through transmission, training, infrastructure, cooking practice by efficient and climate friendly cooking solutions.
3. **Clean Cooking Solutions (CCS):** We continue to promote the use of modern technologies and reduce the use of traditional biomass.

The program targets poor households of the rural, peri-urban and urban areas with specific consideration for women and children. The program will be implemented in the next 5 years. The project seeks a solution up approach to address the needs of defining their household energy needs and the ability to provide clean cooking solutions. This includes the use of modern technologies and the ability to provide clean cooking solutions. The program is designed to support the development of clean cooking solutions.

The program aims to use the Clean Cooking Solutions by implementing the outcomes of (i) Risk leveraging data driven solutions (based on based technology) for carbon efficiencies; (ii) Output-based financing technology ensures sustainable CCS services and drives innovation; (iii) Output-based financing technology ensures sustainable CCS services and drives innovation.

## Fuel accessibility and affordability for ICS

Production will be made based on socio-economic viability indicators such as fuel income, distance to the fuel, fuel costs, household energy needs, social impact, environmental impact, and economic feasibility. The average annual income range between $250-$800. The zone details have been further broken down into:

- **Savannah** and **forest zones**: fuel is collected from LCB with a selected number of households as beneficiaries.
- **Wet savannah** and **forest zones**: fuel is collected from PFAF, Forestry Commission Process, and Private companies awarded by AFC for fuel harvesting and sold to the proposed facility.

Local Community Based (LCB) fuelwood will be purchased in small quantities. The selection criteria will be based on social impact, environmental impact, and economic feasibility indicators. Fuelwood will be collected from households within a 10 km radius of the proposed facility. The average annual income range between $250-$800.

The 20% will be financed by the GCF and AE. A solid verification process will be integral to this financing arrangement. ## Activity 1.4: Monitoring and Verification of Installed GCF funds for Output-Based Financing (OBF)

The project will follow a similar methodology as applied currently in the BAI projects, where the vendor will install and commission equipment at households. The vendor will be responsible for monitoring and reporting on the performance of the installed technologies. The vendor will submit data to the AEPC, which will conduct monitoring of the installations using the data provided by the vendors. The AEPC will also provide a substitute assessment of the level of performance in the cases of non-compliance. In this process, AEPC will verify the installation and operation of the technology by the households after the installation. The BAI model are designed to ensure that the technologies are installed correctly and are operating as intended. As such, they are suited to collect more detailed data resulting in a failure to achieve their installation targets.

The proposed OBF approach would provide output capital for vendors, giving them leverage to service the loan from the financial institution. The vendor will be responsible for the verification of the technologies, which will be conducted by an independent third party. The verification process will ensure that the technologies are installed correctly and are operating as intended. The specifications of the required technologies will be thoroughly listed. Moderate additional amount and time will be needed to conduct this verification. 40% of the funding from the LC will be made once the technologies are verified.

The vendor, local government, and LC will install GCF technologies at the selected households. Simultaneously, a natural fire party monitoring process will be conducted parallel to distribution of the technologies. The monitoring process will include (i) technical assessment of the installations; (ii) validation of the performance of the technology; and (iii) integration of the monitoring system into the existing system. The vendor will also be responsible for the installation, maintenance, and commissioning of the installed systems. The vendor will also be responsible for the verification process.

## Activity 2.1.9 Update existing standards and set benchmarks for the technologies required for the project implementation

This existing standards on GCF will be updated and benchmarks will be set for the technologies required for the project implementation. The standards will be developed in consultation with the relevant stakeholders. Where standards have been set previously, these standards will be reviewed and updated.

AER has had detailed discussions with IEA and other international organizations to develop a set of guidelines for coal fired burning coal stoves “Nepal Interim Guidelines for Clean Coal Combustion” which are being used by the government and private sector. These guidelines have been developed in consultation with the Clean Coal Combustion (CCC) program, national and international experts. NREL and other organizations have also developed standards for clean coal combustion technologies. These documents will be catalogued for future use.

For GCF, the standard will be developed to cover different types of stoves, including induction, infrared, electric, gas, wood, biomass, solar, etc. The standards will include efficiency, emissions, safety and performance criteria. The standards will be developed through the involvement of experts from the relevant ministries, government agencies, private sector and other stakeholders. The standards will be reviewed periodically to ensure they remain current and relevant.

It is also being noted that the NEA will set the benchmarks for the technologies required for the project implementation. The NEA will be the main agency responsible for developing the standards and setting the benchmarks. The NEA will work with the relevant ministries, government agencies, private sector and other stakeholders to develop the standards.

The NEA will also be involved in the development of the guidelines for the clean coal combustion technologies. The NEA will work with the relevant ministries, government agencies, private sector and other stakeholders to develop the guidelines.

## Activity 3.1.3: Captivating Local Governments and Provincial Governments

The project will work with local governments (LG) to build capacity, provide technical assistance, and to facilitate appropriate policies and mechanisms. The project will coordinate with the Provincial Governments to create an enabling environment for the project if approved funds are well supported by the strengthening of local government systems. This will include the development of a framework for LG engagement in climate change adaptation and mitigation planning.

Also, the project will assist LGs in placing dedicated personnel related to environmental management and governance in CCS projects and in conducting annual sustainability monitoring and evaluation. This will ensure that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently. The project will also support local governments to develop and implement a climate change adaptation plan and to promote sustainable development through the project implementation process. The learning and sharing of best practices among LGs will be facilitated.

The project will build capacity of LGs to engage with their local Community Forest User Groups to ensure that they are able to participate in the project effectively.

### Activity 3.1.4: Annual national level review and consultation meeting

An annual national-level review and consultation meeting will be held to discuss progress, challenges, and lessons learned from the previous year's activities. This will provide an opportunity for all stakeholders to review the progress achieved and targets and project milestones are reviewed. See accompanying annexes for detailed information on the objectives of the annual review.

### Output 3: Increased awareness and outreach to enhance benefits

#### Outputs:

- A total of 600 climate change awareness activities conducted in each local government area.
- 750 awareness campaigns conducted (Once at each LG per year for five years).
- 100% of the project's target beneficiaries are aware of the project and its objectives.

#### Activities:

- Conducting community meetings, workshops, and promotional activities in collaboration with LGs, community champions, non-government organizations, and other stakeholders to increase awareness on the benefits, technology, services, and financing of clean cooking solutions. The activities will be conducted through various media channels including radio, television, print, and social media.

## Activity 3.1.1: Induce clean cooking mobilizers in each LG

At least 150 CCS mobilizers, preferably women, will be established in the project area. The CCS mobilizers will be responsible for promoting clean cooking solutions and in developing local business associations and capacity development programs for respective LGs.

The selection criteria for clean cooking mobilizers includes:

- A minimum of a primary education.
- Experience in community mobilization/campaigning and renewable energy promotion.

### Output 3.1. Strengthening service centers, business manufacturers in accurate quality control

#### Details

- **Output 3.1.1 Training provided**: Basic maintenance is conducted with Control for Technician Education and Vocational Training (CTET) institutions on repair and maintenance. Technicians will be trained to provide services to the local population.

- **Output 3.1.3 Service centers established and strengthened**: 900 local service centers are established and strengthened with at least six service centers per district.

One of the main objectives of this project is to strengthen technical capacity and local CCS supply chain industry. The project aims to strengthen each element of CCS supply chain (production, installation, maintenance, repair, and disposal) through:

- **Output 3.1.4 Training provided**: Basic maintenance is conducted with Control for Technician Education and Vocational Training (CTET) institutions on repair and maintenance. Technicians will be trained to provide services to the local population.

### AFOLU (Agriculture Forestry Other Land Use) and GCF
The set of proposed activities are defined based on experiences and learnings from the past. With a view to ensuring that local communities are fully engaged in the process, the project will include a series of consultations with local communities. This will include an ethno-sociological study of the local communities. Furthermore, the proposed process involves a focus on women’s empowerment and gender equality. The project will also include a focus on the inclusion of women and youth in the implementation of the technology.

Similarly, the development of project to pilot a withdrawal of GCF funding will be for a period of 1 year. This will be followed by a further period of 2 years to ensure that the local communities are fully engaged in the process. The project will also include a focus on the inclusion of women and youth in the implementation of the technology.

### Renewable Energy
The Government will be provided with an amount equal to the cost of the renewable energy system installed under this project. This will be used for the development of a set of renewable energy systems that are designed to meet the needs of the local communities. The project will also include a focus on the inclusion of women and youth in the implementation of the technology.

# Internal and Third Party Monitoring Agency

Direct monitoring of delivery of CCS will be done by an independent third party agency. This agency will be hired for qualitative and impact monitoring. The third party will specifically assess and verify implementation percentage of the total beneficiaries in terms of:

- Quality and standard of the CCS technologies.
- Adoption of the CCS technologies during cooking and LPG use.
- Adaptation benefits in terms of vulnerable communities and remote areas, among others.

## Project Beneficiaries

The project beneficiaries are the ultimate target groups of the project and will include services from private sector providers and CCS and CC technologies. Further, the beneficiaries will be categorized into three groups:

1. Vulnerable communities who are highly dependent on biomass for cooking.
2. Communities with high vulnerability and built resistance to climate change impacts through the same sources.
3. Communities who have been identified as vulnerable and in need of replacement fuels for cooking. Further, delivery of project beneficiaries and the selection criteria will be carried out in close coordination with Project Implementation Beneficiaries.

## Overview of Project Funding and Implementation Arrangements

### Key Entities Involved in the Project

- **Green Climate Fund (GCF)**
- **Government of Nepal**
  - Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation (MoEWR)
  - National Planning Commission (NPC) Secretariat
  - Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DoHM)

### Project Implementation Structure

1. **Project Management Unit (PMU)**
   - Located within the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DoHM)
2. **National Designated Authority (NDA)**
3. **Executing Entity**
4. **Accredited Entity**

### Funding Flow

- GCF funds will be transferred to the Government of Nepal through an Inter-Governmental Agreement (IGA) with the Green Climate Fund.
- The Government of Nepal will open a separate bank account for project funds and a separate procurement plan.
- A Project Management Committee, headed by the Secretary of MoEWR, will oversee implementation.
- Procurement plans will be approved by the Project Management Committee based on the needs of the service providers.

## Implementation Arrangements

For financing the project, GCF will enter into the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with AEP as an executing entity. The Government of Nepal's financial system, while procurement procedures and fund flow mechanism will be managed by the Department of National Parks and Wildlife Conservation (DNPWC). The separate bank account will be opened for the project purpose and a separate procurement plan will be developed. The management of the financial resources will be done through the Project Management Committee headed by the Secretary of MoEWR.

## efficiency, AEPC's activities include renewable energy policy formulation and development, and capacity building for local governments to promote climate change mitigation and adaptation. It is also promoting Low emission development (LEDS) in collaboration with UNDP and UN-HABITAT.

AERC has implemented several programmes and projects in coordination with various development partners:

1. **Rural Energy Development Programme (REDP)** - Phase II: 8.65 of 2 Million USD in collaboration with DFID.
2. **Biogas Support Programme (BSP)** - Phase III: IV & V of 2 Million USD in collaboration with KfW.
3. **Energy Efficient Appliance Programme (EEAP)** - Phase III & IV: 84 of 94 Million USD in collaboration with Denmark, Norway, WB, ADB, DFID, UN-HABITAT.
4. **Climate and Carbon Programme** - Phase I & II: 16.54 Million USD in collaboration with EU.

### B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Government of Nepal is seeking support from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to address climate change mitigation and adaptation challenges. The country's vulnerability to climate change impacts necessitates increased investment in climate-resilient infrastructure and sustainable development initiatives.

Government of Nepal investment independence cannot address the climate change mitigation and adaptation challenges alone, and therefore requires external support. The Government of Nepal has been working with various international partners such as UNDP, ADB, DFID, KfW, EU, World Bank, and others to implement climate change projects in the country.

The GCF support will help to network for supporting vulnerable communities. Lower Tar rivers are within its reach to the 37 ICZM however they cannot offer to pay the capital requirement to switch to renewable energy sources. They do not have access to banking facilities to borrow money. In addition, there is no loan products available from commercial banks and microfinance institutions to support the improved CC strategies, which will also facilitate community-based initiatives.

The GCF funding request aims to support the Government of Nepal's efforts to address climate change mitigation and adaptation challenges by providing financial resources for projects that promote renewable energy, energy efficiency, and climate-resilient infrastructure. The funding will be used to implement projects that are aligned with the country's nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and other international commitments.

The GCF funding request is based on a comprehensive assessment of Nepal's climate change vulnerabilities and needs, as well as an analysis of the country's capacity to address these challenges. The proposed projects have been identified through a consultative process involving government agencies, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders.

The GCF funding request is in line with the Government of Nepal's commitment to the Paris Agreement and other international climate change agreements. The funding will be used to support the implementation of the country's NDCs and other climate change initiatives.

## Developing a model of Green Growth for Nepal

The project will contribute to the carbon development pathway by promoting sustainable energy solutions in rural communities. The project is designed to reduce greenhouse gas emissions through the adoption of improved cookstoves and solar home systems. This approach is aligned with the objectives of the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and contributes to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will also contribute to the development of a sustainable energy market in Nepal, which is essential for achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement.

### 3. Sustainable Development Goals (approx. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including:

- **SDG 7: Affordable and Clean Energy**: The project aims to ensure universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy services by promoting the use of improved cookstoves and solar home systems.

- **SDG 3: Good Health and Well-being**: By reducing indoor air pollution, the project will improve health outcomes, particularly for women and children who are most affected by indoor air pollution. This is achieved through the use of cleaner cooking solutions.

- **SDG 13: Climate Action**: The project contributes to climate change mitigation efforts by reducing greenhouse gas emissions from traditional cooking methods.

### 4. Environmental Impact (approx. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to a reduction of indoor air pollution and improve air quality. On average, ICS can reduce fuel consumption by 30%, and ICS and SHS can completely replace the use of kerosene lamps and traditional biomass fuels. The adoption of improved cookstoves (ICS) is a primary source of timber fuel savings.

In addition, the project will replace traditional Tawa for cooking with LPG, which can be used to operate various household appliances. This improves fuel efficiency and reduces carbon emissions. Using clean cooking in domestic kitchens will help mitigate carbon dioxide emissions, contributing significantly to environmental health.

The use of improved cookstoves (ICS) and solar home systems (SHS) will also reduce indoor air pollution, which is a primary cause of respiratory diseases such as pneumonia, bronchitis, and other lung-related illnesses. The project aims to improve the quality of life for rural communities by reducing indoor air pollution and improving access to clean energy sources.

The project will contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions by promoting the use of renewable energy sources in Nepal. It also reduces the prevalence of respiratory diseases caused by indoor air pollution, particularly among women and children.

The NDA is responsible for coordinating with the GCF Secretariat to ensure that the knowledge, processes, and procedures related to GCF affairs are properly followed.

The role of Nepal NDA is as follows:

1. Coordinate with the government to develop a national strategy that is aligned to national priorities for channeling the GCF resources.
2. Conduct consultations with stakeholders to identify country programs and prioritize country programs in areas of GCF funding.
3. Provide technical assistance, capacity building, and training on GCF processes, accreditation, and financial standards forms to engage in raising awareness on GCF processes, capacity development needs, documenting lessons learnt, and facilitating the exchange of information among stakeholders.
4. Facilitate the coordination process for national institutions and provide Necessary Letters to the accredited entities to access the GCF resources.
5. Provide technical assistance to the government in preparing proposals and the implementation of GCF funded projects/programs in Nepal by bringing on-board experts to align funding proposals with the country's priorities.
6. Provide backstopping on the deployment of address and properly update funding in Nepal.

It is also further noted that several qualitative factors have been identified which affect the meaning decision from the household perspective. These include the cost of capital, the availability of credit and the perceived risk of investing in energy efficiency measures. Vulnerability to disruptions and Energy Security: As Nepal has no internal sources of petroleum or natural gas, its vulnerability is enhanced as imports of fuel are the principal reason for the high cost of electricity. The high cost of electricity is a major factor that affects the national energy security. Energy demand in Nepal has increased for a decade. This impact imposed to the households and the economy at large. The government needs to ensure the availability of energy supply and reduce the cost of energy. Hence, proper strategies are required to overcome this pressure crisis. As per the BPC (Board of Power Corporation) and national grid, the average cost of electricity is NRs 10.5 per unit. The average cost of electricity in Nepal is higher than the average cost of electricity in India which is around Rs 6.5 per unit. The government needs to ensure the availability of energy supply and reduce the cost of energy. Hence, proper strategies are required to overcome this pressure crisis. As per the BPC (Board of Power Corporation) and national grid, the average cost of electricity is NRs 10.5 per unit. The average cost of electricity in Nepal is higher than the average cost of electricity in India which is around Rs 6.5 per unit.

Power and income level: The households' ability to pay household energy efficiency investments is largely dependent on their income levels. The majority of the population lives below the poverty line, with a large number of households living below the poverty line. A separate analysis team reported that in Nepal, 80% of the population lives below NRs 10,000 per month, followed by 15% of the population living between NRs 10,000 to 20,000 per month and only 5% of the population living above NRs 30,000 per month.

Remittances: It is expected that the employment sector will be negatively impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic and the return to normal activity may take some time. This presents a significant problem for households who rely on remittance income to pay their energy bills. Remittance income is a major source of household income in Nepal, with an estimated 30% of the population relying on remittances as their primary source of income.

## Activity Update Standards and Procedures

The existing standards on CCS will be amended to include new technologies for post-combustion capture, and the implementation of CCS in cement kilns.

### Existing and New Standards
- Existing and new standards are set for the technologies.
- The standards are reviewed and updated. It is recommended that the standards and procedures are reviewed with "New Electricity" project team to facilitate the structure for electric cooling and heating technologies to meet the capacity of electric power generation in domestic and international markets.

### Strengths/Weaknesses
- Strengths: The existing standards on CCS will be amended to include new technologies.
- Weaknesses: The existing standards are not set for the technologies. The standards are reviewed and updated. It is recommended that the standards and procedures are reviewed with "New Electricity" project team to facilitate the structure for electric cooling and heating technologies to meet the capacity of electric power generation in domestic and international markets.

### # One (1) Resource Person
- Resource person will be identified from the guidelines on CCS.
- #Three (3) Manuals
  - Manual with procedures on CCS.
  - Manual with procedures on electric cooling and heating technologies.
  - Manual with procedures on electric power generation in domestic and international markets.

- The existing standards on CCS will be amended to include new technologies for post-combustion capture, and the implementation of CCS in cement kilns. Existing and new standards are set for the technologies.
- The standards are reviewed and updated. It is recommended that the standards and procedures are reviewed with "New Electricity" project team to facilitate the structure for electric cooling and heating technologies to meet the capacity of electric power generation in domestic and international markets.

## Stakeholder consultation workshops with POs and LGs

### Workshop between AECP, PG and LG

- **Objective**: To develop monitoring plans for Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of LGs to support monitoring by POs.
- **Approach**:
  - Identify key indicators using a participatory approach.
  - Integrate these into existing government systems and include them in LG development plans.
  - At proposal level, 4 energy units will be supported with MRV.
  - At implementation level, the number of LGs supported by CCS will be increased to one. CCS facilitates the integration of MRV into LG development plans.

### Activity: 3.1.2

#### Objective:

- **LG**: Enhance local level for energy monitoring and reporting systems.
- **PO**: Develop a common framework for monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of LG activities, including indicators to measure progress towards achieving the objectives of the project.

#### Outputs:
- **Identity**: Establishes a baseline for engagement from a cross-section of stakeholders.
- **Establish**: Energy Sub-Committee Team to take the lead in developing and monitoring progress across all energy units.
- **Energy**: Develops baselines, targets, vision, mission and goals and associated indicators.
- **Funding source**: Covers costs and funds MEP.

#### Outputs:
- **LG**: 130 LG

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 86 OF 111

## Activity 3.2.3: Identifying clean cooking champions

To identify the need and effectively address gender inequality and social inclusion in clean cooking, the following activities will be carried out:

- **Carry out a stakeholder mapping exercise** to identify key individuals and organizations at the local level to identify the relevant institutions and organizations involved in clean cooking technology promotion and use. This will also be vital for identifying potential champions.
- **Conduct a needs assessment survey** to understand the need, interest, and willingness of households to adopt clean cooking technologies. This will help in identifying potential champions.
- **Develop a gender-responsive behavior change communication strategy** that respects LGs (Local Governments) and community norms and values. This will also be vital for understanding the barriers and opportunities for adopting clean cooking technologies.

### Activity 3.2.4: Enhancing local languages

To enhance local languages, the following activities will be carried out:

- **Translate key materials** into local languages to ensure that all stakeholders understand the activity and its objectives.
- **Develop a communication strategy** in collaboration with LGs (Local Governments) and community leaders to promote clean cooking technologies. This will also be vital for understanding the barriers and opportunities for adopting clean cooking technologies.

### Terms of Reference (ToR)

1. **Report on the identification and motivation of 80 clean cooking champions at LGs**
2. **Disseminate information on local FM (Finance Minister) economic models to promote clean cooking technologies.**
3. **Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) materials highlighting the benefits of clean cooking technologies will be distributed through LGs.**

### Activity: Establish Strengthening Service Centers and Local Level
- **Objective:** To establish service centers at the local level.
- **Description:**
  - **NA** will develop a sustainability model to strengthen local service providers.

## E.7 Monitoring reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Include the arrangements in e.g. normal performance reports sent to AMA, provide a summary of key indicators and targets, and describe how these will be reported on. Describe Arranged Entity (AE) project monitoring arrangements including its alignment with the GCF’s Monitoring Reporting and Verification (MRV) requirements. Describe how the AE will ensure that the arrangements are consistent with the GCF’s MRV. This should include the frequency of reporting on project indicators.

MERC has set up a Monitoring and Quality Assurance (MQA) Unit within the Technical Support Component, which is responsible for setting up an output-based M & E system. One of the major tasks of this unit will be to develop a monitoring framework that covers the monitoring of environmental (climate change as well as non-climatic impacts including Gender and Social Inclusion (GESI).

The MQA Unit has been testing out a necessary M&E systems and tools such as baseline, indicators, data collection methods, etc. The MQA unit is also responsible for developing an integrated web-based MIS as another vital instrument so that everyone involved in the project can have access to the information.

## IV. Impact Assessment

1. The project will assess the quality of access provided applying scientific methodology by developing a multi-faceted impact assessment tool using both qualitative and quantitative methods. This will include:
   - Assessing the impact on local communities.
   - The cooking situation is in line with CCG metrics, including fuel savings or carbon emissions reductions.
   - The project will be designed to ensure that it is not increasing ventilation and reducing the kitchen's safety. While some households may benefit from improved access to clean fuels, others may face challenges due to quality and reliability of access to cooking energy based on feed-based practices.

2. The project will assess the impact of the project on local communities and data usage, and the subsequent actual reductions of fuel-based use and CO2. This will be measured by developing an impact assessment tool that is used to measure the impact of the project. It will be designed to ensure that it is not increasing ventilation and reducing the kitchen's safety. The impact assessment will be conducted annually.

3. AEPCG will conduct market development activities to enhance sustainability of the project.

4. Project team members will be performed at no fee of the project period to assess appropriate performance of the use of funding in terms of mitigation impact through savings.

5. Regular internal evaluation of the PIP targets against completed work as well as yearly assessments and performance throughout the project period.

6. Future additional co-benefits (including gender specific, peace creation, health impacts) will be assessed and monitored to ensure that they are achieved.

## V. Independent Third Party Monitoring and Verification

AEPCG will use a third party independent agency to monitor and verify CCG technology installed at the household. This first yearly monitoring and verification would be conducted through the project period with an annual report submitted to the AEPCG board and public.

## VI. Evaluation

An independent third evaluation would be conducted within six months after the end of the project period if it is not performed during the project period.

MEP being a solely entity needs to be audited by an independent firm, in line with the Public Procurement Act and Regulation. ### Selected Risk Factor 3
- **Category:** Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Medium

Lack of awareness on adoption of electric cook-stove amongst households.

- **Probability:** Medium

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2 | PAGE 180 OF 195

## Cost of fuel for stoves
- **High**

### Description
For T+4 IC requires pellets or very small size wood, meaning the households would have to further process the regular fuel wood to fit into the T+4 IC. In addition, they may have to buy pellets or wood chips which are more expensive than regular fuel wood. This will increase the cost of living for the households especially during the lean season.

#### Mitigation Mechanism(s)
The project will focus on supplying the T+4 IC that uses locally available resources, wood chip and pellet, and provide training on how to use them efficiently. The project will also promote the use of fuel-efficient stoves. For processed fuel, mostly biomass that costs an amount to be lower in fuel cost. The project will also promote the use of fuel-efficient stoves. If households are members of community-based forest, the project will facilitate to provide free or subsidized wood chips and pellets. This will reduce the cost of living for the households and provide a good technical assistance to lower the fuel consumption cost.

## Selected Risk Factor 9
- **Category:** Cost of fuel for stoves
- **Probability:** High
- **Impact:** High

### Description
The electric Stoves requires specific types of cooking stoves. Consumers will have to buy the utility wood, which is expensive.

#### Mitigation Mechanism(s)
The estimated cost of cooking stoves (i.e., 4,900-15,000 USD) for each 4 stoves and household wiring upgrade. Among the estimation is a lower risk in comparison with existing market rate, but it will be higher than the current market price. The project will provide technical assistance to reduce the cost of the consumer in fuel requirements and it is expected as user contribution, means that users have to manage the utility in their own requirements.

## Selected Risk Factor 10
- **Category:** Cost of fuel for stoves
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Medium

### Description
Power upgrade. Cost of upgrading the electricity system in the household, e.g., from 5 amp to 15 amp to accommodate the electric stove.

## G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (units: 750 words, approximately) 1.5 pages

Environmental and social risk assessment is a critical component of the project. The project will ensure that all environmental and social risks are identified, assessed, and managed in accordance with the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS). The project will follow the following steps for assessing and managing environmental and social risks:

1. **Identification**: Identify potential environmental and social impacts of the project.
2. **Assessment**: Assess the significance of identified impacts using appropriate methodologies.
3. **Mitigation**: Develop and implement mitigation measures to address significant impacts.
4. **Implementation and reporting**: Ensure that the mitigation measures are implemented effectively and report on progress.

The Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will be developed in accordance with the GCF ESS standards. If the proposed project is categorized as a Category A project, it will require an ESMP to be prepared and approved by the Executive Committee (EC). The EC will review and approve the ESMP and ensure that it includes appropriate mitigation measures.

The project will also consider any potential impacts on indigenous peoples and the measures to address these impacts. The project will follow the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ensure that all environmental and social risks are managed in accordance with the policy.

Consider any relevant inputs on indigenous peoples and the measures to address these impacts. The project will be guided by the [GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy](https://www.greenclimate.fund/policy/indigenous-peoples-policy) leading to free, prior and informed consent, pursuant to the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy.

After the evaluation and audit of the project proposal, evidence of the environmental and social risk category in area E:

Environmental and social risk category: AEP did not identify any significant adverse environmental or social impacts associated with the project. The project will be implemented in an area that is already degraded and has been used for similar purposes related to domestic biogas plants such as management of bio slurry, livestock farming, and crop production. The project will not involve any new land use changes or deforestation. In addition, the project will be implemented in Environmental and Social Policy, the project people is categorized as C, as an implementation of the existing technology.

Environmental and social management plan: The submitted environmental and social Management Plan (ESMP) that identifies the potential adverse negative environmental and social impacts associated with the project and provides measures to avoid or mitigate these issues. The ESMP also includes a grievance redress mechanism and E & S monitoring. The ESMP is consistent with the Green Climate Fund Environmental and Social Policy.

Environmental and social risks and impacts: The project focuses on rural communities, it is the rural environment that will benefit from clean energy change, reduced indoor air pollution and time savings for women who no longer have to collect firewood. The project will reduce greenhouse gas emissions from the project area and contribute to national environmental objectives. The project will also provide a significant source of income for local communities.

The direct environmental and social risks from the project implementation is limited to effective management of the slurry from biogas plants, digital and damaged cooking stoves and cookstoves and the potential for increased use of firewood. These impacts are minimal and can be effectively managed through proper planning and monitoring. The project will involve women who have been long disadvantaged to bring later to the biogas plant that has a direct impact on their lives.

Indigenous people: Ten indigenous individuals are considered endangered in Nepal, of which 50% live in Terai. Dhime, Rajbanshi groups are present in Terai and Terai is the target area for this project. The project will not affect the indigenous peoples' land or territories as the project aims to enhance their livelihoods through improved access to clean energy. The project will also contribute to the conservation of biodiversity and reduce gender conflicts over resources.

Community health and safety: The project contributes to community health safety by reducing air pollution from indoor biomass burning, which is a major cause of respiratory diseases in Nepal. The project will also improve the health and safety conditions. End user awareness training needs to be included through following a safe use manual.

### Indirect risks and impacts
There are potential indirect risks and impacts of the project across the country. These include changes in fuel prices, which may increase fuel costs for households far into the future. This will need to be measured through the project’s impact evaluation that is regularly assessed as changes the fuel wood use households.

### Stakeholder consultation
In the course of developing the project, stakeholder consultations were conducted with local communities and other stakeholders including government officials, private sector groups, academics and research firms. The results of these consultations have been used to refine the project design and incorporate recommendations for the improved project. Over 150 people have been consulted through various means including face-to-face meetings, focus group discussions and written submissions. These consultations were conducted in the project activities including stakeholder engagement plans and are provided in [APPENDIX-4](#).

### Grievance redress mechanism
A grievance redress mechanism for the GCF project has been developed to ensure that complete information is available to all stakeholders on how to access the grievance redress process. This will be implemented by the project team, along with the support of local government and community leaders. The Project GRM will be based on [GRIEVANCE](#) and will include a complaint box at the field level.

## 2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project contains a comprehensive gender assessment and gender action plan to ensure that the environmental benefits of GCF Gender Play and Action Plan. It also includes a gender action plan (GAP) to ensure that the project is designed to be gender-responsive.

The gender assessment was done based on interviews, reviews, field surveys and stakeholder consultations. This assessment provides in-depth information on the cooking sector with respect to women’s participation in fuel wood collection, use of improved cookstoves, access to finance, and different phases of implementation. Cooking is almost entirely carried out by women in rural areas. Each household day for cooking uses an average of 10 kg of fuel wood.

The gender assessment also provides information on the amount of time spent in collecting fuel wood and how this affects their income. Like many other countries, the amount of time spent in collecting fuel wood is over 75% among women households. In some cases, it is as high as 25%. The value of time lost in collecting fuel wood is estimated to be around $10 per day.

Like many other countries, the amount of time spent in collecting fuel wood is over 75% among women households. In some cases, it is as high as 25%. The value of time lost in collecting fuel wood is estimated to be around $10 per day.

## G.4 Disbursement of funding proposal

### Note:
The Distribution Disbursement Policy (DDP) provides that the GCF will apply a disbursement in excess of 50% only after an information and document review meeting is held with the executing entity and the funding proposal is approved by the GCF. The DDP also requires that the executing entity submits to the Secretariat a request for disbursement that may be disbursed pursuant to this policy. The Secretariat will assess whether the information provided by an entity disburses the distribution contains specific, appropriate portions that can provide evidence of the use of funds.

# Ministry of Finance

## Government of Nepal

### Kathmandu, Nepal

---

**Ref: ECM/MOF/GCR/2078/21/435**

To:
The General Secretary, Federal Parliament Secretariat,
Singha Durbar, Kathmandu.

Date: 25 December 2020

Subject: Funding approval for the GRC by Alternative Energy Promotion Center (AEPC) integrating Grid emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean energy technologies in Nepal.

Dear Madam/Sir,

We are writing to you with reference to our project "Integrating GRC emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean energy technologies in Nepal" submitted to the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC) for its approval and funding support. We would like to bring to your kind attention that the Government of Nepal has approved the project as per the letter dated 13 December 2020 from the Ministry of Finance, Financial Commissions Department.

The objective of the project is to integrate GRC emission through modern, efficient and climate friendly clean energy technologies in Nepal. The project will be implemented by AEPC with the financial support of the Government of Nepal.

We would like to confirm that our application process for availing no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

Yours sincerely,

[Signature]

Secretary/Financial Secretary

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP172

## Proposal name:
Mitigating GCF emission through sources, efficiency and climate smart agriculture in Nepal

## Accredited entity:
Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC)

## Country(ies):
Nepal

## Project (progamme) size:
Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
The project shows a high co-benefit outcome through sources, efficiency and climate smart agriculture in Nepal. The project has been designed to address both GCF approved priorities and the country’s NDC.

The project has high national relevance and support from the Government of Nepal, as well as strong engagement with a number of 150 local communities. The project has high co-benefits including in terms of gender equality and social inclusion through the use of different technologies, as well as strong stakeholder involvement. The project has been approved above an already ambitious GCF Board threshold.

### Points of caution
The project action will be scaled up on the basis of the results from the pilot phase. The project is expected to present actions developed support from local communities and national authorities in partnership with the private sector for project implementation.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background
Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change and is ranked as such in the Global Climate Risk Index (GCRI) 2021. The country has a high level of poverty, with 85% of the population living below the national poverty line. Nepal is also one of the most affected countries by climate change, with an estimated loss of US$ 647 million per year due to climate-related disasters.

### 2.2 Project description
The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture and forestry sectors in Nepal through the implementation of climate smart practices. The project will support the adoption of improved technologies and practices for agriculture, including the use of energy-efficient equipment, sustainable farming methods and agroforestry systems. The project will also promote the development of renewable energy sources, such as solar and wind power, to reduce dependence on fossil fuels.

### 2.3 Project objectives
The main objective of the project is to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture and forestry sectors in Nepal through the implementation of climate smart practices. The specific objectives are:

- To promote the adoption of improved technologies and practices for agriculture, including the use of energy-efficient equipment, sustainable farming methods and agroforestry systems.
- To develop renewable energy sources, such as solar and wind power, to reduce dependence on fossil fuels.
- To support the development of climate smart policies and strategies at the national level.

### 2.4 Project implementation
The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an initial phase of one year for planning and preparation. The project will be implemented by the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC) in partnership with local communities and national authorities. The project will also involve the private sector to ensure sustainability and long-term impact.

### 2.5 Project results
The expected results of the project are:

- A reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture and forestry sectors in Nepal through the implementation of climate smart practices.
- An increase in the adoption of improved technologies and practices for agriculture, including the use of energy-efficient equipment, sustainable farming methods and agroforestry systems.
- The development of renewable energy sources, such as solar and wind power, to reduce dependence on fossil fuels.
- The support of the development of climate smart policies and strategies at the national level.

### 2.6 Project budget
The total budget for the project is US$ 10 million, with an initial phase of US$ 2 million for planning and preparation. The remaining budget will be used to implement the project over a period of five years.

The project area has been identified as a part of the Eastern Ghats and the Western Ghats. The area has been recognized for its high biodiversity value and traditional knowledge in the field of herbal medicine, agriculture, and forestry. The area is also known for its rich cultural heritage and traditional knowledge systems closely linked to natural resource management. The project will be implemented through a participatory approach involving local communities, NGOs, and other stakeholders. The project will focus on the conservation of biodiversity and the sustainable use of natural resources.

The total budget for the project is USD 15 million, of which USD 2.1 million is required from a grant. The remaining amount will be provided by the Government of India (GoI) through various schemes and programs. The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an annual budget of USD 3 million.

The project will be implemented in two phases: Phase I (2017-2019) and Phase II (2020-2025). Phase I will focus on the development of a comprehensive plan for the conservation of biodiversity and sustainable use of natural resources. Phase II will focus on the implementation of the plan.

The project will be implemented through a participatory approach involving local communities, NGOs, and other stakeholders. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government of India (GoI) and other relevant agencies.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

This component involves preparation and implementation of action plans for sustainable use of natural resources (Component 1), development of a comprehensive plan for conservation of biodiversity (Component 2), and development of a comprehensive plan for sustainable use of natural resources (Component 3). The project will involve preparation and implementation of action plans for sustainable use of natural resources, development of a comprehensive plan for conservation of biodiversity, and development of a comprehensive plan for sustainable use of natural resources. The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an annual budget of USD 3 million.

# Green Fund Project

## II. Project Components

### a. Under the component, national level technical standards and documents will be prepared for the GIS technologies proposed in this project. The output of this component will include:

- **GIS technical standards**
- **GIS technical manuals**

The project implementation unit (PIU) will demonstrate government and private sector capacity to develop and adopt these standards. This will also enhance the ability of the government to formulate and develop quality assurance mechanisms for promoting GIS technologies.

### b. Enhancing project components, including management surveys and analysis, and strengthening public awareness mechanisms including establishing a center and conducting training programs.

### c. Governmental, non-governmental organizations, academic circles, charities and international bodies will be involved in this process according to their needs and abilities.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact Potential

The project has an enormous impact potential of 5.5 million tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) over a period of 20 years. This will lead to emissions reductions of approximately 17,178 tons per year (t/y). The project will also contribute to the development of GIS technologies and their application in various sectors such as:

- **Transport**
- **Agriculture**
- **Water supply**

This will help create employment opportunities and increase income. The project will also contribute to the development of a national GIS database and provide training for local communities.

### 3.2 Paradigm Shift Potential

The project has been making efforts to shift towards clean technologies for sustainable development. It is expected that this project will lead to a significant change in the use of GIS technologies by:

- **Government**
- **Private sector**

This paradigm shift potential is estimated at 17,178 tons per year (t/y). The project will also contribute to the development of a national GIS database and provide training for local communities.

# Sustainable development potential

This project contributes to the sustainable development goal (SDG target 7), by 2030 ensuring universal access to affordable, reliable and modern energy services. The project will address one of the indicators, share of the population with access to electricity, by increasing the number of people who have access to electricity from 65% in 2018 to 90% by 2030. As of this same year, the proportion of the population with access to clean cooking facilities is expected to increase from 47% to 60%. This project will contribute to the achievement of these targets and will be a key component in ensuring universal access to electricity.

The project has high potential to generate benefits beyond reducing air pollution. The project will reduce indoor air pollution by replacing traditional biomass with LPG, which is cleaner and more efficient. The project will also contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions through the replacement of traditional biomass with LPG. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG target 7.2, increasing substantially the share of renewable in the global energy mix.

The project has high potential for general socio-economic benefits as a result of increased access to electricity and clean cooking facilities. The project will increase the availability of electricity and clean cooking facilities, which will have positive impacts on health, education, and economic development. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG target 7.1, ensuring universal access to affordable, reliable and modern energy services by 2030.

# Green Climate Fund

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient

### a.
Togo is a least developed country. Today, the project location has a very high population density and is characterized by a low level of education and limited access to basic infrastructure services such as electricity, water supply, sanitation, and health care.

The project will contribute to reducing LPG usage by about 90,000 tons, and saving CO2 emissions by approximately 3,225 tons per year. The project will also reduce the use of firewood for cooking purposes, thereby contributing to forest conservation and biodiversity protection.

### b.
The project has defined beneficiary selection criteria at a high level. The criteria include:

- Households with no access to electricity
- Households with no access to clean cooking solutions
- Households with no access to safe drinking water
- Households with no access to sanitation facilities

The selection criteria will be defined in more detail during the project implementation phase.

### c.
The project will benefit 15,000 households (75,000 people) directly and indirectly. The project will also contribute to the development of local enterprises and job opportunities for women, youth, and other groups. The project will be implemented in collaboration with local governments and communities during the project implementation phase.

## 3.5 Country ownership

### a.
Togo is a signatory to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and has ratified the Paris Agreement. Togo has also submitted its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) in accordance with the UNFCCC process.

### b.
Togo has a total of 7,938,000 hectares of land, of which 25% is used for agriculture. The country has a population of approximately 8 million people and an annual GDP of around $6 billion.
