### 3.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness

The funding proposal was developed with full consultation among all relevant stakeholders.

- The project exhibits high efficiency (10% of GCF funding) in comparison with the overall average of the GCF portfolio, which is around 2%. This is a significant achievement considering that the project was launched in 2019 and the first disbursement was made in 2020. The funding amount represents the 4th largest amount paid out of the ten projects approved by the GCF in 2019.
- The project has been designed to support the government's priority areas, with an estimated 33% going towards Economic Diversification and Employment (ED&E) which also addresses climate change adaptation, health, biodiversity and ecosystem services. These results are robust to the sensitivity analysis.
- The project is expected to deliver significant co-benefits in terms of climate change mitigation and adaptation, as well as economic development.

First, improved cookstove technology shows a significant net present value in the domestic sector. The average household saves USD 80 per year, with an additional USD 15 per year gained through reduced health costs. This is a conservative estimate since the actual benefits are expected to be much higher. The RRR (the reduction rate of greenhouse gas emissions) is estimated at 30% for the project.

Second, the project has been designed to support the government's priority areas, with an estimated 33% going towards Economic Diversification and Employment (ED&E) which also addresses climate change adaptation, health, biodiversity and ecosystem services. These results are robust to the sensitivity analysis.

Third, the project is expected to deliver significant co-benefits in terms of climate change mitigation and adaptation, as well as economic development.

In addition to these benefits, the project has been designed to support the government's priority areas, with an estimated 33% going towards Economic Diversification and Employment (ED&E) which also addresses climate change adaptation, health, biodiversity and ecosystem services. These results are robust to the sensitivity analysis.

# Green Climate Fund

## Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project is classified as Category B by the AF which the Secretariat considers to be within all applicable safeguard requirements. The project will not result in adverse environmental or social impacts that are significant, irreversible or unprecedented. The project will not require any additional safeguards beyond those required for the category of the project. The project will be subject to the following safeguards:

- Environmental and social management plan (ESMP) to ensure compliance with the safeguard requirements.
- Regular monitoring and reporting on environmental and social impacts.
- Compliance with relevant national laws and regulations.

The environmental and social safeguards include reduced indoor air pollution, improved health outcomes for women and children, and increased access to clean cooking solutions. The project will also contribute to the following safeguards:

- Gender equality: The project will promote gender equality by providing women with access to clean cooking solutions.
- Indigenous peoples: The project will respect the rights of indigenous peoples and ensure their participation in decision-making processes.
- Community health: The project will improve community health outcomes through reduced indoor air pollution.

The project has documented and provided a summary of the consultations undertaken, including how the safeguards were integrated into the design and implementation of the project. The project has also documented how it will address any potential adverse impacts that may arise during the life of the project.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and thereby complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, and will be carried out in collaboration with the National Institute of Nutrition, Hyderabad, and the Indian Council of Medical Research, New Delhi. The project is designed to address the following objectives: (i) to assess the impact of smoking on offspring development; (ii) to determine the effects of exposure during pregnancy on offspring development; (iii) to identify the mechanisms by which tobacco smoke constituents affect fetal development; and (iv) to develop a strategy for reducing the risks associated with smoking in pregnancy. As an AE, AEP will receive the funds from GCF and separate bank account will be opened in the project country. The funds will be transferred to the implementing entity (IE) through the AE. The AE will develop guidelines for the management of the blended finances.

### 4.4.1

In its capacity as EL, AEPC will set up a dedicated FU to ensure and manage the project portfolio. The FU will be responsible for the overall management of the projects in the portfolio. The FU will prepare an annual work plan and budget including a procurement plan, which will be approved by the GCF Board. The FU will also develop guidelines on the use of funds, accounting and reporting systems, and procurement procedures. The FU will also provide support to IEs in developing their own internal systems.

### 4.4.2

The AEPC will be in accordance with the OPR of the Financial Compliance Committee. Backed by AEP, a government entity within the national host of AEPCs will serve as the secretariat for the committee. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP172

**Proposed name:** Mitigating GHG emissions through modern efficient and climate-friendly clean cooking solutions (ECS)

**Accredited entity:** Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC)

**Country(ies):** Nepal

**Project/programme size:** Small

## I. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential
Nepal’s contribution to global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions is very small, but the country is highly vulnerable to climate change and its impacts. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Program in Nepal has identified clean cooking as a priority area for climate action. The proposed project aims to promote the adoption of improved cookstoves (ICS) and biogas technologies in rural communities. The project will be implemented by the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC), which has extensive experience in promoting ICS and biogas technologies.

The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting the use of efficient and clean cooking solutions. The project will provide financial support to households for the purchase of improved cookstoves and biogas systems, as well as training and awareness-raising activities. The project is expected to result in significant reductions in GHG emissions, as well as improvements in public health and gender equality.

The proposed project is aligned with the objectives of the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) of Nepal and the Paris Agreement. The project will contribute to the achievement of Sustainable Development Goal 7 (SDG 7), which aims to ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all. The project will also support the achievement of other SDGs, including SDG 3 (good health and well-being) and SDG 5 (gender equality).

The project is expected to have a high impact potential, as it addresses a critical need in Nepal and has the potential to significantly reduce GHG emissions while improving public health and gender equality.

The programme is currently implemented in 10 districts with a total population of approximately 60 million people. # preserving (financial aggregation and bulk tendering approaches), implementing and monitoring renewable energy technologies projects and programmes,

## 01

and promoting the development of a green economy. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a new financial mechanism that has been established to support developing countries in their efforts to address climate change. The GCF will promote a paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development, while increasing the ability of developing countries to cope with the adverse impacts of climate change. The GCF will be guided by the principles and provisions of the Convention, including those related to equity, and will aim to maintain a balance between mitigation and adaptation. The GCF will also promote gender equality, environmental and social safeguards, and will ensure that projects are implemented in a transparent and accountable manner.

## 02

The GCF is governed by a Board composed of 24 members, with an equal number of representatives from developed and developing countries. The Board has the overall responsibility for the management of the Fund, including the approval of funding proposals, the monitoring of funded projects, and the provision of strategic guidance to the Secretariat.

## 03

The GCF is designed to be a catalyst for climate action, providing funding to developing countries for mitigation and adaptation projects. The GCF will support projects that are aligned with national priorities and contribute to the achievement of nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement. The GCF will also support projects that are consistent with the principles and provisions of the Convention, including those related to equity, gender equality, environmental and social safeguards.

## 04

The GCF is a key component of the international climate change regime, and its success is critical to the achievement of the goals of the Paris Agreement. The GCF will provide funding for projects that are aligned with national priorities and contribute to the achievement of nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement.

## 05

Actions and strategies that aim to address the identified barriers and achieve a successful transition to a low-carbon economy have been incorporated into the country preparedness programmes. These include:

- **Barrier 1**: Lack of awareness and understanding of climate change risks.
- **Barrier 2**: Limited access to finance for climate change projects.
- **Barrier 3**: Inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks.
- **Barrier 4**: Lack of capacity and technical expertise.

## 06

To address these barriers, the country preparedness programmes include a range of activities aimed at raising awareness, building capacity, and providing access to finance. These activities are designed to support the development of climate change projects that are aligned with national priorities and contribute to the achievement of nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement.

## 07

Adopting an integrated approach that has a focus on youth engagement and empowerment is critical for the success of these programmes. This includes:

- **Activity 1**: Conducting awareness-raising campaigns targeted at young people.
- **Activity 2**: Providing training and capacity-building opportunities for young people.
- **Activity 3**: Establishing mentorship programmes that connect young people with experienced professionals.

## 08

The country preparedness programmes also include a range of activities aimed at promoting the development of climate change projects that are aligned with national priorities. These activities are designed to support the achievement of nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement and contribute to the goals of the Convention.

# cost and health benefits of ICS through campaigns and promotional actions, treating gender parity and environmental issues.

## Three phases of the project

### Phase 1: Pilot campaign in a 5-year period with a strong focus on pre-implementation activities to ensure the success of the project.
- Changes coupled with interventions in other basic services increase the impact of the campaign.
- Training and support with enhanced repair and maintenance services.

### Phase 2: Implementation of the CCS project in an independent manner, including:
- Reinforcement of community-level capacity building, such as capacity county governments to implement the project.
- Development of technical expertise on LPG, stoves, and fuel-efficient cookstoves.
- Strengthening of existing test centers to establish the quality of the products.

### Phase 3: Addition of gender issues on technological aspects (strongly focused development of women's involvement in relevant components of the campaign).

## Promotional activities
- Electric cooking in good conditions households through LPG and fuel-efficient cookstoves.
- Training of local artisans and technicians to promote the use of LPG and fuel-efficient stoves for household consumption.
- Information and awareness campaigns on the benefits of using LPG and fuel-efficient stoves.

## Gender and environmental issues
- Gender and environmental issues will be included in all activities.
- Women's participation in the project will be encouraged through training, support, and marketing.

## Rural Renewable Energy Laboratory (RREL) and household energy mapping as that the electricity utility can upgrade, agencies and organizations involved in the project will work with NGA on increasing distribution of LPG to rural areas.
- Through the proposed project, the government will play a major role in promoting clean cooking.
- The project will be implemented by the CCS program in order to distribute the usage of LPG in the country.

## Actions
- Promote the use of LPG and fuel-efficient cookstoves through marketing campaigns.
- Encourage households to adopt cleaner fuels and technologies for cooking.

- North Central
- North East
- North West
- South East
- South South
- South West

# Component 2: Strengthening enabling environment through stakeholder assessment and spatial awareness of the technologies

## Activity 2.1: Develop partnership agreement between AEFI, provincial governments, local government, and other implementation partners.
- Establish a national task force to oversee the implementation process for the project.

## Activity 2.2: Develop a monitoring strategy using indicators by analyzing needs.
- Monitor and evaluate the impact of the technologies on the enabling environment.

## Activity 2.3: Strengthen and establish testing centers for ensuring quality of the technologies.
- Ensure that all testing centers are equipped with the necessary tools to conduct accurate tests.

## Activity 2.4: Develop a management information system as a national strategy.
- Implement an MIS to track progress and ensure accountability.

# Component 3: Enhancing "inclusiveness" capacity for the supply chains and ensuring demand-driven access to clean cooking solutions

## Activity 3.1: Develop a national task force agreement will include:
- Establish a national task force to oversee the implementation process for the project.

## Activity 3.2: Conduct national stakeholder consultation workshops.
- Organize workshops to gather input from various stakeholders and develop an action plan.

## Activity 3.3: Capacity building for local governments and provincial governments.
- Provide training and resources to local and provincial governments to enhance their capacity in implementing clean cooking solutions.

## Activity 3.4: Annual national best practice review and consultation meeting.
- Conduct annual reviews to assess progress and identify areas for improvement.

## Activity 3.5: Establish clean cooking monitoring indicators.
- Develop a set of indicators to monitor the effectiveness of clean cooking initiatives.

## Activity 3.6: Develop guidelines for training materials.
- Create comprehensive training materials for stakeholders involved in clean cooking projects.

## Activity 3.7: Develop materials for awareness (CC) (as national and local languages) with an input potential in households and support vulnerable groups.
- Develop awareness materials in local languages to reach a broader audience, particularly vulnerable groups.

## Activity 3.8: Conduct and promote activities in collaboration with the PSO, NGOs, and other stakeholders.
- Collaborate with various organizations to enhance the impact of clean cooking initiatives.

## Activity 3.9: Improve monitoring material and guidance.
- Provide improved monitoring materials and guidelines for better tracking and evaluation.

## Activity 3.10: Capacity building and training service centers at provincial and local levels.
- Conduct capacity-building workshops for service centers at various levels to enhance their effectiveness.

## Activity 3.11: Establish a national task force agreement will include:
- Establish a national task force to oversee the implementation process for the project.

## Activity 3.12: Conduct training in preparation of clean energy and fuel manufacturing in collaboration with financial and technical partners.
- Provide training and support for the establishment of clean energy and fuel manufacturing facilities.

## Activity 3.13: Establish a national task force agreement will include:
- Establish a national task force to oversee the implementation process for the project.

# A robust quality assurance mechanism

- Objectives: Strengthening quality assurance mechanisms. This will consist of:
  - Establishing and strengthening a quality chain mechanism for CFC.
  - Ensuring that all CFC activities are carried out in accordance with the established standards, and
  - Strengthening at least two testing centers at central level, and

- Objectives: Capacity development of institutional institutions. This will consist of:
  - A comprehensive work plan developed by each institution,
  - At least one training conducted on quality assurance mechanisms [1 technical and 1 administrative] and at least 150 CFC technicians allocated at local level, and
  - At least two training centers established in collaboration with the local government with priority given to CFC.

## Outputs

### Output 3.1: Strengthened measures and structures to enhance demand

- A total of 450 clean cooking champions identified, three in each local government area,
- 720 awareness campaigns conducted (one in each LG per year for the five years),
- Cleanliness of people and communities enhanced with thorough savings and awareness of climate change adaptation measures, and
- At least two training centers established at central level.

### Output 3.2: Quality assurance mechanisms strengthened

- 24 training provided to beneficiaries in collaboration with CFCY technicians on quality assurance mechanisms,
- 24 practical field trainings provided to service centers in collaboration with CFCY technicians (one per year from year 1 to year 5),
- Five performance service centers established and strengthened, and
- At least two testing centers established at central level.

### Output 3.3: The quality assurance mechanism enhanced

The quality assurance mechanism will be developed by the intervention of the assigned project. The quality assurance mechanism will be developed in collaboration with the local government and the local communities. The quality assurance mechanism will be based on the following principles:

- 0.11 million tons of CO2e reduced during the lifetime of the technology replacing LPG stoves,
- The Independent TAF concludes that this project has high climate impact potential.

# 1.2. Paradigm shift potential

## Score High

### I.S.
The paradigm shift potential of the CCS project in Nepal is discussed considering the extent to which the project can catalyse impacts beyond the project intervention. Previous studies have shown that the success of a project depends on the ability to address the root causes of poverty and ensure certain conditions must be made for the process. Potential paradigm shifts are discussed below.

### I.I.A. Paradigm shift in the preconditions process
As standard practice, inherent barriers have prevented the achievement of expected results in the preconditions process. The project has been able to overcome these barriers through a participatory approach and community-based monitoring. In the project area, the CCS project has been able to address the root causes of poverty by addressing the underlying issues that affect the preconditions process. This paradigm shift will enable the project to achieve its objectives more effectively.

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Proposal

### Job creation - About 2,400 people will be trained in part of this project: provide services to CCS providers and local manufacturers, maintain and operate equipment, and perform other duties.

### Social benefits - The project will create jobs for local communities, improve the quality of life through increased income, and reduce the greenhouse gas emissions. It will also supply clean water to local communities, enhance food production, boost nutrition, strengthen health services, and improve educational opportunities.

### Health benefits - The project will provide clean drinking water from rainwater harvesting systems. The project proposes that the best adapted crops for the region will be planted in the demonstration farm. This will provide income and food security for businesses involved in CCS projects.

## Time gained from income generating activities and education

### Women who suffer the most from climate change are women farmers, who spend more time on farming and less time on other activities. The project aims to reduce the time spent by women farmers on farming and increase their participation in income generating activities and education. This will free up their time for other activities.

## Safety at the national level from LPC exploitation

### LPC is one of the hardest cooking fuels, which causes respiratory diseases such as asthma, bronchitis, and pneumonia. The project aims to reduce about 30,000 tons of LPC emissions per year by replacing traditional biomass with LPG. This will improve the health of women and children. The project also aims to reduce deforestation by 17.5 hectares a year, thus reducing the trade deficit.

### LIA - Social and health co-benefits

#### The project will have significant social impacts that are expected to be achieved through the CCS project. These include:

- Improved access to clean water.
- Reduced respiratory diseases.
- Improved food security.
- Increased income for local communities.

### LIA - Environmental co-benefits

#### The project is expected to deliver the following environmental benefits:

- Reduction of indoor air pollution.
- Reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.
- Reduction of forest degradation.

### LIA - Gender-related co-benefits

#### Key gender-related development impacts of the CCS project can be summarized as follows:

- Reduced workload and reduced exposure to indoor air pollution.
- Time gained from income generating activities and education, and
- Training and development for women, with regards on existing traditional gender norms.

### Development - The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) concluded that the project will generate significant development impacts. These include:

- Improved access to clean water.
- Reduced respiratory diseases.
- Reduced exposure to indoor air pollution.

# Needs of the recipient

## Needs of the country

- Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change. The country is ranked as the ninth most vulnerable country in the world according to the Climate Risk Index 2019.
- Nepal’s vulnerability is very marginally to global GGR emissions, the country’s climate policies and strategies are aligned with the Paris Agreement.
- Nepal is committed to holding active actions to mitigate climate change. Nepal is therefore considered to be eligible for support from the GCF.
- Nepal therefore calls on all Partners to increase their ambitions and more collectively address climate change.

### Agreement

Therefore, the proposed GEF intervention is aligning a need of the country for sustainable development with the GEF strategic priorities and the GEF-Accreditation Platform.

## Needs of the implementing agency

The Government of Nepal (GoN) has established the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MoFE) as the focal point to manage climate change issues. The MoFE is responsible for the implementation of the UNFCCC process, including the preparation of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), the development of climate policies and strategies, and the coordination of climate change-related activities.

# Green Climate Fund

## Objectives for climate change mitigation, energy use in households and the built environment, and the energy sector in Nepal

GCF resources from the household sector are expected to be used to support:

- (a) access to sustainable technologies and practices that lead to improved cooking, heating, lighting, transport and storage stations;
- (b) the promotion of renewable energy sources for electricity generation; and
- (c) the development of climate-resilient infrastructure.

### (a) Access to sustainable technologies

The project aims to strengthen technologies and practices that lead to improved cooking, heating, lighting, transport and storage stations. The project will also support the installation of 20,000 improved cookstoves in rural areas by 2023. These cookstoves will be installed through a combination of direct sales and microfinance.

### (b) Promotion of renewable energy sources

The fifth five-year plan of the National Planning Commission envisions the development of renewable energy sources as one of its key objectives. The project will support the development of renewable energy sources in Nepal, with a particular focus on solar power.

### (c) Development of climate-resilient infrastructure

The White Paper 2018, prepared by the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation, envisions direct access to every house and building as a prerequisite for achieving the government's objectives. The project will support the development of climate-resilient infrastructure in Nepal.

### Engagement with all relevant stakeholders and other relevant sub-national governments

The funding proposal was developed with full consultation of all relevant stakeholders, including representatives of religious peoples.

### Capacity-building activities

The project will include a comprehensive capacity-building program to ensure that the project is implemented effectively. The project will also support the development of a national climate-resilient infrastructure strategy and action plan.

### Capacity of the implementing entity and key partners

As part of the funding proposal, the executing agency (APDC) and executing partner (GEE) for this project will be APDC and the Government of Nepal acting through the Ministry of Energy, Water Resources, and Irrigation. The national budget of APDC is US$ 5 million per year.

### Implementation arrangements

The project will be implemented by APDC in collaboration with GEE. The project will be implemented over a period of five years, from 2018 to 2023. The total cost of the project is expected to be US$ 50 million.

# 1.6. Efficiency and effectiveness

## 1.6.1 Financial structure

The proposed project is expecting grant funding from GCF and co-financing (Loans as well as the cash flow of IAD 45% of the total USD 20 million). The GCF contribution amounts to USD 9.2 million, which is equivalent to 46% of the total project cost. The co-financing from IAD amounts to USD 10.8 million. The project will be implemented over a period of five years with an expected start date in Q3 2017.

The breakdown of the funding contributions from these sources, the type of funds and the use that will be provided for the project is presented in table 3 below:

| Source               | Type of Fund       | Amount (USD million) |
|----------------------|---------------------|----------------------|
| GCF Grant            | Non-repayable grant | 9.2                  |
| IAD Co-financing     | Loan                | 10.8                 |

## 1.6.2 Co-financing arrangements

The total fund required from GCF is about 42.5% per cent of the total fund required for the project.

The grant funding from GCF has been earmarked in follows:

- About 63 per cent of the GCF grant funding will be spent on the activities of component 1.
- About 37 per cent of the GCF grant funding will be spent on the activities of component 2.

Of the GCF grant 80 per cent has been earmarked for activities under Component 1, which will cover capacity development of institutional infrastructure, technical assistance and training, monitoring and evaluation, and community awareness. The remaining 20 per cent will be used to cover the activities under Component 2, which will cover capacity development of institutional infrastructure, technical assistance and training, monitoring and evaluation, and community awareness.

## 1.6.3 Co-financing: Leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment

# I. Project Overview

1. The USD 2 billion request from GCP will be complemented by USD 28 million from MoG through a mechanism - APF (USD 21.2 million) and the UNDP TAD (7 million). This project is part of the GCP's overall strategy to support the implementation of the National Development Plan (NDP) model, which can be considered an effective use of funds for GCP.

2. Other key performance issues of the financing scheme can be summarized as follows:
   - Approximate 7% cost on disbursement, hence will be provided by APF, with the remaining 93% from the UNDP TAD.
   - GCP financing ensures the project viability and enables the effective use of the leveraged funds.

3. A financial analysis showed that the project can deliver a financial internal rate of return (IRR) of 12%, which is higher than the discounting profit rate of 7%.

4. The financial analysis also estimated an economic internal rate of return (EIR) of about 8% per annum, which is higher than the discounting profit rate.

5. GCP funding and financing scheme will ensure that the project will be feasible as the NPV is positive (USD 86.4 million).

6. The independent TAD's of the project showed that the financial performance required by GCP can be achieved with a reasonable level of risk, which is acceptable to GCP.

## II. Cost Affordability

The cost affordability analysis shows that the USD 2 billion cost of GCP's overall investment requirements in Ethiopia will be affordable if the disbursement pattern that will be adhered to this project intervention were:

1. Total project financing + USD 45.2 million.
2. Requested GCP amount + USD 21.2 million.
3. Expected disbursement GCP amounts + USD 13.6 million (APF) and USD 7.6 million (UNDP TAD).
4. Estimated GCP cost per year (GCP = USD 90/1) - 1.28 USD / OB.mg.

The independent TAD's conclude that the GCP will be able to meet its financial obligations of the project with a reasonable level of risk, which is acceptable to GCP. There is a possibility that there may be inadequate electricity supply from the national grid and the project may not be implemented as planned. The project will be implemented in a timely manner by the government and the private sector. The project will be implemented by the government and the private sector with a reasonable level of risk, which is acceptable to GCP.

# Introduction

## 1 Gender Background

The Constitution of Nepal (1975) and the United Nations Declaration for Women (1976) both were a start for bringing women into development mainstream. For the first time, the government recognized the need to empower women in the country. The National Women's Development Plan (1980), followed by the National Action Plan for Women Development (1988-1998), and the National Policy on Women (1997) were formulated. The Constitution of Nepal (2063) abolished the discrimination against women in all forms of discrimination against women (ICRW). After this, the government has been working to empower women through various programs.

The Government of Nepal promulgated in 2015 with the commitment to a gender-equal society by guaranteeing rights and opportunities for women. The government has also committed to ensuring equal participation of women in all sectors including politics, economy, social, culture, and environment. The Constitution of Nepal (2063) provides for the protection of women's rights.

Women Development Program (WDP), started in 1985, is a national development effort with a focus on empowering rural women through income-generating activities. This program includes the development of Women's Development Committees (WDCS). WDCS is a department under the Ministry of Women, Children and Social Welfare (MoWCSW) that works to empower women by providing them with opportunities for self-reliance.

International Monetary Fund (IMF), in 2017, mentioned that Nepal has made significant progress in gender equality. The government has also been working on various programs to promote the rights of women and girls. The government has been working on various programs to promote the rights of women and girls. The government has been working on various programs to promote the rights of women and girls.

Nepal Demographic and Health Survey (NDHS) had conducted a survey in 2016 which provides information about the status of women empowerment in Nepal. The survey found that there are still many challenges facing women in Nepal. The survey also found that there is a need for more programs to promote the rights of women.

a. Employment: 84% of married women were employed in part-time jobs compared to 97% of men.
b. Overhead of assets: 36% saw a home, and 15% had land or property, among others.
c. Education: 20% of women have completed secondary education.

Source: [IMF](https://www.imf.org/en/Publications/WEO/Issues/2017/pr/04/24/nepal-women-empowerment-programme)

# c. Participation in household decisions (57%) to make decisions about major household purchases

Separate government programmes such as The President’s Women Empowerment programme have been introduced to promote women empowerment and gender mainstreaming and promoting women long-standing agenda of empowerment and emancipation. These programmes are part of the government’s women empowerment programmes included at national and local levels.

The majority of federal affairs and local development had introduced Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Policy 2074 as an initiative to empower women in decision-making processes. The National Plan of Action for Women has included the budget of NRS 15 billion to mainstream gender issues and promote gender equality. The government system of the country has been promoting gender equality and social inclusion from 2013/2014 to 2017/2018.

The recent Ten-Year Plan has the gender equality and women empowerment vision to “Nepal where all women live with dignity, enjoy equal rights, and have opportunities to develop their full potential”. The plan is aligned with reference to related data from 2014/2015 to 2018.

### 1.2 Gender and Energy Sector

Normally energy policies are designed from a technical and gender neutral perspective and have not been able to address the issues of gender equity and social justice. The health, gender education opportunities, household environmental cleanliness and sanitation can be affected by the availability of energy services. However, integrating these issues into the energy sector planning is essential for achieving sustainable development goals.

Major policy documents governing the energy sector include the Electricity Act 1992, Hydropower Development Policy (1993), the National Water Resources Strategy (NWRS) 2005, and National Plan for Action on Climate Change (NPACC). The legal and policy framework governing energy sector is largely based on recognizing GESI as a factor in regulating operating licenses, with user categories in e.g. Rural Energy Policy, 2006. A number of policies have been developed to address the issues of gender equity in the energy sector. For example, the Renewable Energy Subsidy Policy, 2005, has been revised to GESI issues for women; the Renewable Energy Act, 1992; for instance, the Rural Energy Policy, 2006, among others, explicitly states "the rural energy policy aims at ensuring access to modern energy services and technologies for all, including the poorest and most vulnerable groups in the country's remote areas." The policy also emphasizes the importance of gender equity in the energy sector. Policies which are more relevant to the group components would be Renewable Energy Subsidy Policy (2005), Rural Energy Policy (2006) and National Water Resources Strategy (NWRS).

a. Additional subsidy of Rs. 1,500 per household will be provided to households with single women.

b. The Government of Nepal has also introduced a policy for the provision of free electricity connection to the poorest families in the country. This policy was introduced in 2013 and will be provided if a woman is the head of the family, or if she is the sole breadwinner, or if she is a widow.

c. For cooking and heating purposes, traditional stoves of up to NRs 6000 and IPP 800 are subsidized for women's groups.

The National Rural Renewable Energy Programme (NRREP) managed by AEPC is a five-year programme that aims at providing renewable energy services to the rural population. The programme has been implemented in 53 districts of Nepal since 2014.

# Resources Needed to Set Up Microfinance Institutions

Gender stereotypes for women to have less education, early marriage, and total responsibility for household activities limit their ability to participate in microfinance. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes within households, which restricts their access to credit and limits their ability to engage in income-generating activities.

There is no path to gender equity without addressing the issue of unequal access to financial services. There is a global loan gap of $30 billion for women entrepreneurs. Women face higher interest rates on loans from informal sources than men do, and they are more likely to be denied credit by formal lenders. Women also have less collateral and fewer assets to secure loans.

The Gender Equity in Microfinance (GEM) initiative is a partnership between the Grameen Foundation and Women's World Banking to promote gender equity within microfinance institutions. GEM provides training, technical assistance, and advocacy support to help microfinance institutions address gender issues and improve their services for women clients.

Women face higher interest rates on loans from informal sources than men do, and they are more likely to be denied credit by formal lenders. Women also have less collateral and fewer assets to secure loans.

Honduras: Napoli Microfin Bank directs its loans and financial inclusion programs to the banks and financial institutions regulated by the National Banking Commission (CNV). The CNV has a gender policy that requires all banks to develop and implement policies to promote equal access to credit for women. The CNV also provides training and technical assistance to help banks comply with these requirements.

Most organizations and MFI have more than 50% female members. Women head households in over 90% of the cases. Therefore, these organizations are providing credit (financial empowerment) for women who are the main breadwinners in their families. This has led to increased cooperation and networking among women for micro-entrepreneurship.

Credit financing through microfinance organizations has been proven to be a great source of small businesses. Few examples of loans to purchase electric cookstoves in Kenya did not have the expected impact because they were not accompanied by training on how to use them. The Grameen Foundation and other organizations have developed better Capital expenses of cleaner cookstoves. This will create a conducive environment to enable higher utilization.

## 1.2.2 Gender and Cook Stove

Women are the primary users of wood fuel for cooking in rural areas, which is the main source of household air pollution. Women spend an average of two hours per day collecting firewood and suffer from respiratory illnesses as a result. The use of cleaner cookstoves can reduce indoor air pollution by 90% and reduce the time spent collecting firewood.

# Gender Assessment

## 1. OCR Results for Gender Assessment

The DPs commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive country programmes and initiatives that benefit everyone, women and men. This ensures gender equality understanding the needs of both genders and the potential contribution of women and men to sustainable peace and development. The project promotes equal access to resources and opportunities in climate change adaptation and mitigation, and promotes gender equality in climate funding by mainstreaming gender in the project implementation process. It also aims to promote a climate change through sustainable management of natural resources (MAE), Gender mainstreaming through the climate project expects from project definition MAE.

## 2. Gender Assessment

The project is implemented in 10 districts, covering 4,889 households with a total family size of 4-6 people per household. In four regions, 31% of direct beneficiaries are female. The direct beneficiaries of the project comprise about 50% of total population, with 23% of them being male and 77% female. The indirect beneficiaries account for about 50% of total population.

The DPs are the most vulnerable community as they are among the poorest and socially excluded groups. 23.4% of the population in the five regions where climate change projects are implemented, including 18.9% of women and 6.7% of men. The project is implemented in 50 districts with a total population of 25 million people.

## 2.2 State of Gender Equality

### i. Social norms and practices

Social norms significantly constrain gender inequality. Traditionally, it is widely practiced to give preference to boys over girls in education, employment, and inheritance. Women are often denied the right to own land or property, which is considered a male domain. Women have limited access to decision-making processes, including local governance structures, which hinder their ability to participate fully in community development activities. Women also face discrimination in the workplace, which limits their ability to have equal opportunities as men. The women's empowerment index shows that women are less likely to be employed than men and have lower levels of education. Working women and women with young children have good access to the project activities and benefits.

*Source: 2015 Nepal Living Standard Survey (NLSS) 2014/2015*

# Major Initiatives and Policy Priorities

## Education, Workforce Development, and Economic Opportunity

Education, workforce development, and economic opportunity are relatively new, wide labor force participation, low unemployment, and population's commitment to even higher levels of that labor participation. Policies that promote education and training, particularly for those who have been displaced by technology or global competition, are essential. The state has witnessed a significant increase in the number of students enrolled in postsecondary education. In 2018, over 300,000 students were enrolled in postsecondary institutions, an increase of nearly 50% since 2009. The state also has seen an increase in the number of students graduating from high school and enrolling in college. In 2018, over 70% of high school graduates enrolled in college within one year of graduation.

### Policy Enforcement

Graduates have been made to participate more equitably and social justice in all the sectors. Integration of GIS policies upon Professional Level has opened up an employment to the students. The state has also taken steps to promote equal opportunities for all students, regardless of their race, gender, or socioeconomic status. The state has also implemented policies that promote diversity and inclusion in the workforce. Social policy arguments reports and reviews submitted to the legislature and the governor's office have been used to inform policy decisions. The state has also implemented policies that promote economic development and job creation. The state has also implemented policies that promote social justice and equality.

Women's labour force participation rate, employment status and social class in the labour market show their economic status has improved. Yet the labour market remains marked by many traditional barriers and forms of deep-rooted discrimination.

The labour force participation rate is general or women's role as a particular form of employment. The overall female labour force participation rate was 50.3 percent, compared to 81.2 percent for men. Of the total number of employed women, 47.6 percent were in full-time jobs and 39.7 percent in part-time work. Among those working as employees, only 22.9 percent of women were in managerial positions, compared with 50.8 percent of men.

Participation in formal and informal economies

Nearly half of all women work for more than two employers, spending 25 to 35 hours a week on household tasks, domestic and agricultural activities. Yet, due to the nature of their labour, women are often not counted as economically active or employed. Women's economic participation remains relatively invisible, stemming from the most profound forms of discrimination and exclusion. This is particularly true in rural areas where women participate actively in the market economy.

The role played by women in the care sector, predominantly in the reproductive work, bearing the burden of unpaid labour, has been systematically undervalued. Women's activities as a crucial factor for household maintenance are often not considered as economic activity. This is particularly true when their work is not remunerated and hence not monetarily valued; women's time and energy are not counted as economic and hence monetarily valued.

Education

Gender gaps in the education sector have been narrowing over the past decade, with girls now outnumbering boys in secondary schools. The percentage of women participating in primary education has increased substantially in the last two decades, where the number of girls enrolled in primary school is almost equal to that of boys. However, the gender gap remains significant in tertiary education, especially in rural areas where studies have found that girls are less likely to be enrolled.

Marriage

Early and forced marriage is a feature that continues to limit the opportunities for women's empowerment. Despite being illegal, child marriage is still widespread in many countries.

### iv. Health

Reproductive health has long been funded by the government. Each local government has a health department that provides reproductive health services. The government has also established a maternal and child health program to promote maternal and child health. Travel and shelter allowance is provided to pregnant women who are in labor, and the husbands of pregnant women receive travel allowances.

# Gender Action Plan (GAP)

GAP forms the basis for operationalising the results and recommendations of the gender assessment. It incorporates gender elements during project planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. The GAP is aligned with SDG5 gender policy and AEPC’s Gender Equality Strategy.

The GAP is designed with the involvement of the host countries and planned staff and experts. Additionally, the programme will continue the positive and negative effects of the program activities by having a greater relations by setting up a dialogue, gender sensitive monitoring and collecting sex-disaggregated data. The programme will also ensure that all publications (including peer reviews) and evaluations are gender-sensitive. AEPC’s Gender Equality Strategy will execute GAP in their program management capacity.

## Objectives: Promote gender equality and women’s empowerment through capacity building, awareness raising, and advocacy

### Activities 2.1.1 Methods for monitoring and evaluating gender mainstreaming activities at the APEC level

# Recommendations

## Actions used for connection of measures to gender equality objectives

### 1. Strengthen women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels and include gender perspectives in policies and programmes

- Ensure a gender approach to women's participation in decision-making processes, including the appointment of women and men to key positions.
- Increase women's participation in local government bodies, boards, and other decision-making structures.

### 2. Ensure equal access to women to economic resources and opportunities

- Ensure equal access to women to economic resources and opportunities, including property rights and financial services.
- Promote women’s entrepreneurship and business development through targeted support programmes.

## Recommendations for the use of gender equality indicators in other gender-related implications

### 3. Use of enterprises’ sex-disaggregated data on employment and working conditions (Healthy progression)

- Collect sex-disaggregated data on employment, wages, and working conditions from enterprises.
- Use this data to monitor progress towards gender equality objectives.

### 4. Gender-responsive budgeting (Economic empowerment)

- Implement gender-responsive budgeting to ensure that public resources are allocated in a way that promotes gender equality.
- Conduct regular training for government officials on how to use sex-disaggregated data and gender analysis tools.

### 5. Use of enterprises’ sex-disaggregated data on employment and working conditions (Economic empowerment)

- Collect sex-disaggregated data on employment, wages, and working conditions from enterprises.
- Use this data to monitor progress towards gender equality objectives.

### 6. Use of enterprises’ sex-disaggregated data on employment and working conditions (Economic empowerment)

- Collect sex-disaggregated data on employment, wages, and working conditions from enterprises.
- Use this data to monitor progress towards gender equality objectives.

# Annex 1: Stakeholder consultation Report

## Sub-national Stakeholder Consultation Review for “Mitigating GHG emissions through modern, efficient and clean cooking solutions (ECS)”

APEC held a workshop program on 27th June 2020 with various stakeholders and relevant institutions as the proposed project “Mitigating GHG emissions through modern, efficient and clean cooking solutions (ECS)” was presented. The consultation meeting targeted 35 participants from 10 APEC member economies including government officials, private sector representatives, and non-government organizations.

The meeting was facilitated by Dr. Michael Slayman, Assistant Director of AFoCO, who is also the focal point for ECS in the Philippines. Dr. Nona Roco, the Deputy Executive Director of AFoCO, gave an overview of the project and its objectives. The consultation was attended by representatives from various sectors including government officials, private sector representatives, non-government organizations, academia, and other stakeholders with active participation of women, youth, and vulnerable groups.

The main issues raised for discussion included but not limited to:

1. **Role of ECS at local level**
   - Strengthening planning and funding of local governments in ECS promotion including funding mechanisms.
   - The need for capacity building on ECS (including funding sources that can be explored for ECS projects).
   - Key policy analysis for ECS.
   - The importance of local government at local level and provision of incentives to engage them for the project.

2. **ESG indicators and mitigation measures**
   - Indicators for monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) of ECS.
   - The need for capacity building on MRV of ECS at local level.
   - The importance of local government in ECS promotion including funding mechanisms that can be explored for ECS projects.

3. **Health impact Chain**
   - Assessing fuel for cooking.
   - Capacity of local supply chain in meeting the demand.
   - The need to ensure that ECS is local level.
   - Opportunities to improve the supply chain.

### Feedback

- It is very good idea to include Provincial Energy Coordination Committee (PECC) in providing 2, Provines 5 and Kauswagan Province for promoting the demand as this initiative includes the private sector.
- The need to include local government in the project.
- AFoCO promotes clean, AERMOD and MERRA.
- The need to include local government in the household head.

# Green Climate Fund

## Overview

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a United Nations-established fund created to support the efforts of developing countries to respond to the impact of climate change.

### Mission

- To promote a low-emission and climate-resilient development.
- To support the adaptation efforts of developing countries.
- To contribute to the achievement of the ultimate objective of the UNFCCC.

## Key Features

- **Funding**: Provides funding for projects that address both mitigation and adaptation.
- **Partnerships**: Works in partnership with other international organizations, governments, and private sector entities.
- **Transparency**: Ensures transparency and accountability in its operations.

### Funding Mechanisms

1. **Mitigation**
   - Renewable Energy
   - Energy Efficiency
   - Sustainable Transport

2. **Adaptation**
   - Water Management
   - Agricultural Resilience
   - Disaster Risk Reduction

## Contact Information

- Website: [www.greenclimate.fund](http://www.greenclimate.fund)
- Email: info@greenclimate.fund
- Phone: +82 2 2290 5600
